Morse Index Stability of Willmore Immersions I
Alexis Michelat, Tristan Rivière

Abstract
In ( [ 31 ] ), F. Da Lio, M. Gianocca, and T. Rivière developped a new method to show upper semi-continuity results in geometric analysis—which they applied to conformally invariant Lagrangians in dimension 2 2 (that include harmonic maps). In this article, we apply this method to show that the sum of the Morse index and the nullity of Willmore immersions is upper semi-continuous, provided that the limiting immersions and the bubbles are free of branch points. Our result covers the case of degenerating Riemann surfaces for which the ratio of the second residue (considered by P. Laurain and T. Rivière in [ 25 ] ) and the length of the minimal shrinking geodesic of the underlying sequence of Riemann surfaces is smaller than a universal constant.



Mathematical subject classification :
35J35, 35J48, 35R01, 49Q10, 53A05, 53A10, 53A30, 53C42.

1
Introduction

1.1
History and Background

Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface and fix some integer
n\geq 3
. For all immersion
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
g=\vec{\Phi}^{\ast}g_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
be the induced metric on
\Sigma
, where
g_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is the flat metric on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The Willmore energy of
\vec{\Phi}
is the quantity

\displaystyle W(\vec{\Phi})=\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{H}|^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g},

(1.1)

where
\vec{H}
is the mean-curvature vector, given locally by

\displaystyle\vec{H}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}g^{i,j}\,\vec{\mathbb{I}}_{i,j},

(1.2)

where
\vec{\mathbb{I}}_{i,j}
is the second fundamental form of the immersion
\vec{\Phi}
. The Willmore energy, that first appeared in the work of Poisson and Sophie Germain (
[
54
,
16
]
) is conformally invariant thanks to the work of Blaschke and Thomsen in the early
1920
s (
[
7
,
63
]
). In other words,
W
is invariant by isometries, dilations and inversions, provided that the inversion does not modify the topology of the surface
\vec{\Phi}(\Sigma)
. More precisely, the quantity

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{h}_{0}|_{\mathrm{WP}}^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{\Sigma}\left(|\vec{H}|^{2}-K_{g}\right)d\mathrm{vol}_{g},

(1.3)

is conformally invariant for
all
conformal transformations, where
K_{g}
is the Gauss curvature, and
\vec{h}_{0}=2\,\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi},\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi})\,dz^{2}
is the Weingarten tensor, and for all quadratic differential
\alpha=f(z)dz^{2}
and
\beta=h(z)dz^{2}
, we define in a conformal (or complex) chart the
Weil-Petersson product
of
\alpha
and
\beta
by

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{\mathrm{WP}}=\mathrm{Re}\,\left(e^{-4\lambda}f(z)\overline{h(z)}\right),

(1.4)

where
g=e^{2\lambda}|dz|^{2}
.
What about Willmore? He was unaware of the previous work and reintroduced this definition in his
1965
article (
[
66
]
)—and reaped all honours for conjecturing that a particular revolution torus (the Willmore torus, which is no other than the stereographic projection of the Clifford torus, a minimal surface of
S^{3}
) has the least energy amongst
all
tori.
A
Willmore surface
is a critical point of
W
, and classically satisfies the equation

\displaystyle\Delta_{g}^{N}\vec{H}-2|\vec{H}|^{2}\vec{H}+\mathscr{A}(\vec{H})=0

(1.5)

where
\Delta_{g}^{N}
is the normal Laplacian, and
\mathscr{A}
is the Simons operator, such that for all
\vec{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}(\vec{X})=\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}e^{-4\lambda}\langle\vec{\mathbb{I}}_{i,j},\vec{X}\rangle\vec{\mathbb{I}}_{i,j}.

In codimension
1
(if
n=3
), the equation reduces to

\displaystyle\Delta_{g}H+2H(H^{2}-K_{g})=0,

(1.6)

where
\vec{H}=H\,\vec{n}
, and
\vec{n}:\Sigma\rightarrow S^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the unit normal. If
n\geq 3
is arbitrary, then the unit normal
\vec{n}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathscr{G}_{n-2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a map taking values in the Grassmannian space of
(n-2)
-vectors. It is related to the previous tensors as follows:

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}|d\vec{n}|_{g}^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}=2\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{H}|^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}+2\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{h}_{0}|_{\mathrm{WP}}^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{\Sigma}(4|\vec{H}|^{2}-2K_{g})d\mathrm{vol}_{g}.

Thanks to Gauss-Bonnet theorem, the Dirichlet energy of the unit normal is variationally equivalent to the Willmore energy, and if
A
designs the full fundamental form, we also have

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}|A|^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{\Sigma}|d\vec{n}|_{g}^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}.

1.2
Morse Index Stability

In this article, we are concerned with issues of Morse index stability, that are related to min-max problems (
[
58
]
). In particular, a natural development of the theory developed here would be to apply it to the
viscosity method
(first developed in the case of minimal surfaces by T. Rivière
[
59
]
). Due to the extreme technical difficulty associated branched Willmore surfaces (and the length of the present article and the companion paper
[
47
]
), we will concentrate ourselves on the case where the limiting surface does not have singularities. However, notice that the most technical part of the two articles, namely the estimates in neck regions, is proved in complete generality for all order or branch points (
[
47
]
).

Compared to the lower semi-continuity Morse index estimates, upper semi-continuity results are typically much more complicated due to the need of obtaining precise estimates of the sequence of solutions in regions of loss of compactness. The main technical issue that one must needs overcome is that in neck regions, there could be (infinitely many) negative variations. If the underlying Riemann surfaces degenerates and the image geodesics have long enough length, our proof indicates that there could be negative variations located in the neck regions, which would violate the upper semi-continuity inequality.

The strategy in our proof closely follows the approach developed in
[
31
]
by Da Lio-Gianocca-Rivière. Let us explain the main steps of this method, whose flexibility shall become apparent in our article. The approach can be decomposed into four major steps.

1.2.1
Neck Regions

As a means to simplify the exposition of the method of
[
31
]
, we restrict to the simplest example of harmonic maps with values into
S^{3}
. Fix a closed Riemann surface
\Sigma
, and a Riemannian metric
h
on
\Sigma
of constant curvature of unit volume. The Dirichlet energy of a map
\varphi:\Sigma\rightarrow S^{3}
is defined by

\displaystyle E(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}|d\varphi|_{h}^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{h}.

(1.7)

A (stationary) harmonic map is a critical point of
E
for variations of the type
\left\{\varphi_{t}=\pi_{S^{3}}(\varphi+t\,u)\right\}_{-\varepsilon<t<\varepsilon}
, where
u:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{4}
is an arbitrary smooth map.

Let
\left\{\varphi_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}:\Sigma\rightarrow S^{3}
be a sequence of harmonic maps into
S^{3}
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}E(\varphi_{k})<\infty.

Then, by classical results (and up to a subsequence)
\left\{\varphi_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges in
W^{1,2}
towards a macroscopic harmonic map
\varphi_{\infty}:\Sigma\rightarrow S^{3}
and a finite number of
“bubbles.”
In order to simplify the presentation, assume that
\Sigma=S^{2}=\mathbb{C}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
, and assume that there is a single bubble. Then, we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\varphi_{\infty}\qquad\text{in}\;\,C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S^{2}\setminus\left\{0\right\})\;\,\text{for all}\;\,l\in\mathbb{N},

and there exists a sequence
\left\{\rho_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
\alpha>0

\displaystyle\varphi_{k}(\delta_{k}\,\cdot\,)_{B(0,\alpha^{-1})}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\psi_{\infty}\;\,\text{in}\;\,C^{l}(B(0,\alpha^{-1}))\;\,\text{for all}\;\,l\in\mathbb{N},

where
v_{\infty}:S^{2}\rightarrow S^{3}
is a harmonic map. The second derivative of
E
is given by

\displaystyle Q_{\varphi}(u)=D^{2}E(\varphi)(u,u)=\int_{\Sigma}\left(|d\varphi|_{h}^{2}-|d\varphi|_{h}^{2}|u|^{2}\right)d\mathrm{vol}_{h}.

The quantization of energy states that

\displaystyle E(\varphi_{k})\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}E(\varphi_{\infty})+E(\psi_{\infty}).

(1.8)

Equivalently, (
1.8
) holds if and only if

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}|d\varphi_{k}|_{h}^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{h}=0,

(1.9)

where for all
\alpha>0
,
\Omega_{k}(\alpha)=B_{\alpha}\setminus\overline{B}_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k}}(0)
.
Thanks to an
\varepsilon
-regularity result, one proves that for all
x\in\Omega_{k}(\alpha)

\displaystyle|\nabla\varphi_{k}(x)|\leq\frac{C}{|x|}\left\|\nabla\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{k}(2\alpha))}.

(1.10)

As we have said it above, the main part of the proof is to show that the neck regions contribute positively to the second derivative. The first important estimate is a weighted Poincaré inequality.

Theorem 1.1

(Lemma IV.
1
,
[
31
]
)
.

Let
0<a<b<\infty
, and let
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
. For all
u\in W^{1,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\geq\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

Therefore, a trivial estimate shows that for all
u\in W^{1,2}_{0}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))
,

\displaystyle Q_{\varphi_{k}}(u)\geq\left(\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\delta_{k}}\right)}-C\left\|d\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}^{2}\right)\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

We see that the no-neck energy (
1.9
) is not sufficient to show that
Q_{\varphi_{k}}(u)\geq 0
, and (
1.10
) needs to be refined.

1.2.2
Step 1: Lorentz Space Energy Quantization and Pointwise Estimate in Neck Regions

A flexible method to prove energy quantization results was first developed by Lin-Rivière (
[
30
,
29
,
28
]
). It consists in first proving an energy quantization in the weak
L^{2}
space
L^{2,\infty}
(also known as Marcinkiewicz space), and showing that the
L^{2,1}
energy is bounded. The Banach space
L^{2,1}
is a Lorentz space or a special case Orlicz space which is the pre-dual of
L^{2,\infty}
(for more details, refer to the appendix of
[
46
]
). If
\left\{\varphi_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a sequence of harmonic maps such that

\displaystyle\left\|d\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Sigma)}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}0,

and

\displaystyle\left\|d\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Sigma)}\leq C,

then the
L^{2,1}/L^{2,\infty}
duality shows that

\displaystyle E(\varphi_{k})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}|d\varphi_{k}|_{h}^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{h}\leq\left\|d\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Sigma)}\left\|d\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Sigma)}\leq C\left\|d\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Sigma)}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}0.

For a precise definition of Lorentz spaces, see the appendix of
[
46
]
. In this article, we will mostly need the following estimates:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\left\|\frac{1}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=2\sqrt{\pi}\\
&\left\|\frac{1}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0))}=\sqrt{2\pi}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\\
&\left\|\frac{1}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0))}=4\sqrt{\pi}\left(\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)+\log\left(1+\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{2}}\right)\right).\end{aligned}\right.

(1.11)

Generalising
[
31
, Lemma III.
1
]
to Jacobian-type systems, we show the following result, that is proven for a smaller range of exponents
0<\beta<1
in
[
31
]
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
0<2\,a<b<\infty
,
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
, and for all
0<t\leq 1
, let
\Omega_{t}=B_{t\,b}\setminus\overline{B}_{t^{-1}a}(0)
. Let
A\in W^{1,(2,1)}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
B\in W^{1,2}(\Omega,M_{n}(\mathbb{R}))
, and assume that

\displaystyle\Delta A=\nabla^{\perp}B\cdot\nabla A\qquad\text{in}\;\,\Omega.

(1.12)

Let

\displaystyle\Lambda=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\Omega_{1/2}}\frac{|\nabla A|}{|x|}dx.

Then, for all
0<\beta<1
, and for all
0<\delta<1
, there exists
\varepsilon_{\beta,\delta}>0
and a
universal
constant
C<\infty
such that the hypothesis

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla B|^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{\beta,\delta}

implies that for all
z\in\Omega_{1/2}
,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla A\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B_{2|z|}\setminus\overline{B}_{|z|}(0))}\leq C\left(\left(\frac{|z|}{b}\right)^{\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|z|}\right)^{\beta}\right)\left\|\nabla A\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\frac{\sqrt{2\pi\log(2)}}{\log\left(\frac{b}{4a}\right)}\left(1+\delta\right)\left(\Lambda+C\left\|\nabla A\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}\right).

Now, assuming that a strong
L^{2,1}
energy quantization holds, namely

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|\nabla\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}=0,

(1.13)

we deduce by the
L^{2,1}
duality that

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\alpha,k}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|\nabla u_{k}|}{|x|}dx\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\left\|\nabla\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\left\|\frac{1}{|x|}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,\infty}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\left\|\nabla\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))},

which implies thanks to the
\varepsilon
-regularity (
1.10
) that for all
x\in\Omega_{k}(\alpha)

\displaystyle|\nabla\varphi_{k}(x)|\leq\frac{C}{|x|}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\beta}+\frac{1}{\log\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\delta_{k}^{2}}\right)}\right)\left\|\nabla\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(2\alpha))}.

(1.14)

Now, to conclude the proof, one needs to prove a weight Poincaré inequality in neck regions.

1.2.3
Step 2: Weighted Poincaré Estimates in Neck Regions

If one adds the natural weight appearing in the previous inequality from (
3.1
), the constant does not converges to
0
as the conformal class diverges to
\infty
.

Theorem 1.3

(Lemma IV.
1
,
[
31
]
)
.

Let
0<a<b<\infty
and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For all
\beta>0
, there exists a constant
C_{\beta}>0
such that for all
u\in W^{1,2}_{0}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\geq C_{\beta}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\beta}\right)dx.

1.2.4
Step 3: Final Estimate

Fix some
0<\beta<1
. Thanks to (
1.14
) and (
1.3
), there exists
\lambda_{0}>0
and
\lambda_{1}>0
such that for all
\alpha>0
and for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have for all
u\in W^{1,2}_{0}(\Omega_{k}(\alpha))

\displaystyle Q_{\varphi_{k}}(u)\geq\left(\lambda_{0}-\lambda_{1}\left\|\nabla\varphi_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2,1}(\Omega_{k}(2\alpha))}\right)\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{\alpha}\right)^{\beta}+\left(\frac{\alpha^{-1}\delta_{k}}{|x|}\right)^{\beta}+\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\delta_{k}}\right)}\right)dx.

Thanks to the
L^{2,1}
energy quantization (
1.13
), there exists
\alpha_{0}>0
such that for all
0<\alpha<\alpha_{0}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
large enough

\displaystyle Q_{\varphi_{k}}(u)\geq\frac{\lambda_{0}}{2}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\frac{|u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{\alpha}\right)^{\beta}+\left(\frac{\alpha^{-1}\delta_{k}}{|x|}\right)^{\beta}+\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\delta_{k}}\right)}\right)dx,

(1.15)

which finally shows that variations located in the neck regions contribute positively to the second derivative.

1.2.5
Step 4: Sylvester’s Law of Inertia and Final Argument

We see that the estimate in neck region suggests the introduction of a weight
\omega_{k,\alpha}
on
\Sigma
such that

\displaystyle\omega_{k,\alpha}(x)=\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{\alpha}\right)^{\beta}+\left(\frac{\alpha^{-1}\delta_{k}}{|x|}\right)^{\beta}+\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\delta_{k}}\right)}\right)\quad\text{for all}\;\,x\in\Omega_{k}(\alpha).

Interpolating on
\Sigma
with constant non-zero functions, Sylvester’s Law of Intertia asserts that both operators
\mathscr{L}_{g_{k}}=-\Delta_{h}-|d\varphi_{k}|_{h}^{2}
and
\mathcal{L}_{k,\alpha}=-\omega_{k,\alpha}^{-1}\left(\Delta_{h}+|d\varphi_{k}|_{h}^{2}\right)
have the same Morse index. Finally, if
u_{k}
is a negative eigenvalue, the normalisation

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}|u_{k}|^{2}\omega_{k,\alpha}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}=1,

together with a lower-estimate for the smallest eigenvalue (
[
31
, Lemma IV.
4
]
) permits to show that
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
W^{1,2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which permits to extract a converging subsequence. Using the estimate (
1.15
), one proves that the variation cannot be located in neck regions, which permits to link them to negative (or null) variations of the limiting harmonic map or its bubble. This concludes the proof of the main theorem of
[
31
]
.

1.3
Da Lio-Gianocca-Rivière’s Method Applied to Willmore Immersions

1.3.1
Generalities on Willmore Immersions

Fix a Riemann surface
\Sigma
, and let
\{\vec{\Phi}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of immersions from
\Sigma
to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}W(\vec{\Phi}_{k})<\infty.

Furthermore, assume that the conformal class of
\Sigma
is bounded, or more generally, that the condition on the second residue
\vec{\gamma}_{1}
of
[
25
]
holds (see (
1.29
) for an explicit definition).
Then, thanks to Bernard-Rivière work
[
6
]
(or Laurain-Rivière’s
[
25
]
), the energy quantization holds. Assume for simplicity that there is a single
“bubble.”
Then, there exists
p\in\Sigma
and a branched Willmore immersion
\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that (modulo diffeomorphisms in the domain and conformal transformations in the target space) up to a subsequence

\displaystyle\vec{\Phi}_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}\;\,\text{in}\;\,C^{l}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Sigma\setminus\left\{p\right\})\;\,\text{for all}\;\,l\in\mathbb{N},

and a Willmore sphere
\vec{\Psi}_{\infty}:S^{2}=\mathbb{C}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that in a conformal chart
B(0,1)\subset\mathbb{C}
centred in
p
, there exists
\left\{\rho_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,\infty)
such that
\rho_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}0
, and for all
\alpha>0

\displaystyle e^{-\lambda_{k}}(\vec{\Phi}_{k}(\rho_{k}\,\cdot\,)-\vec{\Phi}_{k}(0))_{B(0,\alpha^{-1})}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\vec{\Psi}_{\infty}\quad\text{in}\;\,C^{l}(B(0,\alpha^{-1}))\;\,\text{for all}\;\,l\in\mathbb{N}.

The quantization of energy asserts that

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}W(\vec{\Phi}_{k})=W(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty})+W(\vec{\Psi}_{\infty}).

(1.16)

It is equivalent to the
no-neck energy property
, namely

\displaystyle\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}dx=0.

(1.17)

where
\Omega_{k}(\alpha)=B_{\alpha}\setminus\overline{B}_{\alpha^{-1}\rho_{k}}(0)
is the neck region, the annular region connecting the macroscopic surface to its bubble. Now, for all vectorial variation
\vec{w}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the second derivative of
\vec{\Phi}
is given by (
2.2
) by

\displaystyle Q_{\vec{\Phi}}(\vec{w})=\int_{\Sigma}\bigg\{\frac{1}{2}\left|\Delta_{g}^{N}\vec{w}+\mathscr{A}(\vec{w})\right|^{2}+2|\langle d\vec{w},\vec{H}\rangle|_{g}^{2}-\left(|d\vec{w}|_{g}^{2}-16|\partial\vec{\Phi}\,\dot{\otimes}\,\partial\vec{w}|_{\mathrm{WP}}^{2}\right)|\vec{H}|^{2}

\displaystyle+32\langle\partial^{N}\vec{w}\,\dot{\otimes}\,\partial^{N}\vec{H},\partial\vec{\Phi}\,\dot{\otimes}\,\partial\vec{w}\rangle_{\mathrm{WP}}-8\langle\partial\vec{w}\,\dot{\otimes}\,\partial\vec{w},\langle\vec{H},\vec{h}_{0}\rangle\rangle_{\mathrm{WP}}+8\langle d\vec{\Phi},d\vec{w}\rangle_{g}\langle\partial\vec{\Phi}\,\dot{\otimes}\,\partial\vec{w},\langle\vec{H},\vec{h}_{0}\rangle\rangle_{\mathrm{WP}}

\displaystyle+8\,\{\vec{\Phi},\vec{w}\}_{g}\langle i\,\partial\vec{\Phi}\,\dot{\otimes}\,\partial\vec{w},\langle\vec{H},\vec{h}_{0}\rangle\rangle_{\mathrm{WP}}\bigg\}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}.

(1.18)

The Morse index is the number of negative eigenvalue of the associated fourth-order elliptic operator
\mathcal{L}_{g}
acting on normal variations:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g}=\mathscr{L}_{g}^{2}+4\,d^{\ast_{g}}\left(\langle d(\,\cdot\,),\vec{H}\rangle\vec{H}\right)-2\,d^{\ast_{g}}\left(|\vec{H}|^{2}\,d(\,\cdot\,)\right)+8\langle\langle\vec{h}_{0},\,\cdot\,\rangle,\langle\vec{h}_{0},\,\cdot\,\rangle\rangle_{\mathrm{WP}}|\vec{H}|^{2}

\displaystyle+16\,\ast_{g}\,d\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(g^{-1}\otimes\langle\vec{h}_{0},\,\cdot\,\rangle\otimes\overline{\partial}^{N}\vec{H}\right)-16\,\langle\partial^{N}(\,\cdot\,)\,\dot{\otimes}\,\partial^{N}\vec{H},\vec{h}_{0}\rangle_{\mathrm{WP}}+16\,\ast_{g}\,d\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(g^{-1}\otimes\langle\vec{H},\vec{h}_{0}\rangle\otimes\partial(\,\cdot\,)\right)

\displaystyle+8\langle\langle\vec{H},\vec{h}_{0}\rangle,\langle\vec{h}_{0},\,\cdot\,\rangle\rangle_{\mathrm{WP}}\vec{H}+8\langle\vec{H},\,\cdot\,\rangle\mathrm{Re}\,\left(g^{-2}\otimes\langle\vec{H},\overline{\vec{h}_{0}}\rangle\vec{h}_{0}\right).

(1.19)

Thanks to the identity

\displaystyle Q_{\vec{\Phi}}(\vec{w})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\langle\vec{w},\mathcal{L}_{g}\vec{w}\,\rangle d\mathrm{vol}_{g},

(1.20)

if for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\lambda)=W^{2,2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\left\{\vec{w}:\mathcal{L}_{g}\vec{w}=\lambda\,\vec{w}\right\},

then the Morse index of
\vec{\Phi}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}^{\mathcal{L}}_{W}(\vec{\Phi})=\dim\bigoplus_{\lambda<0}\mathcal{E}(\lambda),

while the nullity is the dimension of the Kernel of
\mathcal{L}_{g}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Null}_{W}^{\mathcal{L}}(\vec{\Phi})=\dim\mathrm{Ker}(\mathcal{L}_{g}).

(1.21)

One of the main technical difficulties or
[
47
]
is to generalise Theorem
1.3
to a family of second-order differential operators. First, using the analysis of
[
46
]
, if
\Omega_{k}(\alpha)
is a neck region, there exists an integer
m\geq 1
such that for
\alpha>0
small enough and
k
large enough,

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{k}}=e^{\mu_{k}}|z|^{m-1}\qquad\text{for all}\;\,z\in\Omega_{k}(\alpha),

where
\mu_{k}
is a uniformly bounded function on
B(0,\alpha)
. Restricting to normal variations
\vec{w}=u\,\vec{n}
, we see that the highest-order term in
Q_{\vec{\Phi}}(u)
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}(\Delta_{g_{k}}u)^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g_{k}}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}e^{-2\mu_{k}}|z|^{2-2m}(\Delta u)^{2}|dz|^{2}.

Now, making the change of variable
u=|z|^{m-1}v
in neck regions, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}(\Delta_{g_{k}}u)^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g_{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}e^{-2\mu_{k}}\left(\Delta v+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla v+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}v\right)^{2}dx

\displaystyle\geq C\int_{\Omega_{k}(\alpha)}\left(\Delta v+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla v+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}v\right)^{2}dx.

Therefore, we are led to study the properties of the second-order elliptic operator with
regular singularities
(
[
49
, (
2.10
) p.
25
]
) whose adjoint operator already appeared in
[
41
]

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}=\Delta+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}},

and more precisely, the first eigenvalue of the fourth-order elliptic operator

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}=\Delta^{2}+2(m^{2}-1)\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}-4(m^{2}-1)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)+\frac{(m^{2}-1)^{2}}{|x|^{4}}.

1.3.2
General Estimates for a Family of Elliptic Fourth-Order Operators

The generalisation of Theorem
1.3
is far from straightforward, and due to length considerations, we prove it in a separate paper (see
[
47
]
). Furthermore, an additional eigenvalue problem needs to be considered in order to treat the derivatives of lower order. Contrary to Theorem
1.3
, the inequality is only verified when the conformal class of the annulus is large enough.

Theorem 1.4

(
[
47
]
)
.

For all
m\geq 1
, there exists
0<\lambda_{m}<\infty
such that for all
0<a<b<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq\lambda_{m},

(1.22)

and let
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then, for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}u\right)^{2}dx\geq\frac{4m^{2}\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx,

(1.23)

and

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}u\right)^{2}dx\geq\frac{\left(4m^{2}+\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}{4(m^{2}+1)+\dfrac{2\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

(1.24)

The second estimate is the generalisation of
[
31
, Lemma IV.1]
(see Theorem
1.3
), which is as expected much more technical (especially for
m>1
).

Theorem 1.5

(Theorem A, Theorem B
[
47
]
)
.

Let
0<a<b<\infty
, and define
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then, for all
\dfrac{1}{2}<\beta<\infty
, there exists
C_{\beta}<\infty
such that for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{4\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{4\beta}\right)dx\leq C_{\beta}\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u\right)^{2}dx

and for all
\sqrt{2}-1<\beta<\infty
, there exists
C_{\beta}^{\prime}<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{2\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{2\beta}\right)dx\leq C_{\beta}^{\prime}\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx.

For all
m>1
and for all
\alpha>0
, there exists
C_{\alpha,m}<\infty
such that for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}+\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\right)\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{2\alpha}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{2\alpha}\right)dx\leq C_{\alpha,m}\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}u\right)^{2}dx.

Therefore, we are brought to define the following weight (where
\dfrac{1}{2}<\beta<1
is fixed once and for all) on any annulus
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
(that we identify to an annulus of
\Sigma
by a fixed chart), that is nothing else than a constant interpolation of the natural weight given by the previous two estimates:

\displaystyle\omega_{a,b}(x)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{b^{4m}}\left(1+\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{4\beta}+\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,x\in\Sigma\setminus\overline{B}_{b}(0)\\
&\frac{1}{|x|^{4m}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{4\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{4\beta}+\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,x\in B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\\
&\frac{1}{a^{4m}}\left(1+\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{4\beta}+\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,x\in B_{a}(0)\end{aligned}\right.

(1.25)

Here, we see additional difficulties arising when
m\geq 2
. Indeed,
\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\delta_{k},\alpha}
converges to
\omega_{0,\alpha}
as
k\rightarrow\infty
, where

\displaystyle\omega_{0,\alpha}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{\alpha^{4m}}\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,x\in\Sigma\setminus\overline{B}_{\alpha}(0)\\
&\frac{1}{\alpha^{4\beta}}\frac{1}{|x|^{4(m-\beta)}}\qquad&&\text{for all}\;\,x\in B_{\alpha}(0)\end{aligned}\right.

Since
0<\beta<1
, provided that
m\geq 2
, our weight
\omega_{0,\alpha}
does not belong to
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Sigma)
. Therefore, normalising an eigenvalue as
1
with respect to our weight will not allow us to take a strong limit in the quantity

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}u_{k}^{2}\,\omega_{\alpha^{-1}\delta_{k},\alpha}\,d\mathrm{vol}_{g_{k}}=1.

(1.26)

This is one of the reasons why in the asymptotic analysis, we restrict to the case
m=1
. Determining the exact (possible) defect in the upper semi-continuity of the (extended) Morse index will be the topic of a future work (
[
45
]
). However, our present analysis permits to treat the case of degenerating Riemann surfaces (
[
25
]
), as in
[
31
]
in the case of conformally invariant problems in dimension
2
—that include harmonic maps.

Before stating our main theorem, let us give a few additional comments the possible extensions of this work.

Although those eigenvalue estimates or weighted Poincaré inequality seem to restrict to dimension
2
, Theorem
1.5
easily generalises to dimension
4
for the Laplace operator thanks to the Sobolev embedding
W^{2,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\hookrightarrow W^{1,4}(\mathbb{R}^{4})\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
for all
p<\infty
. In this example, using the conformal invariance (notice that
\mathscr{L}_{m}
is not conformally invariant in dimension
2
for all
m>1
), the proof is an easy adaptation of the proof of
[
31
, Lemma IV.
1
]
. More surprisingly, Theorem
1.4
generalises in dimension
4
for the first eigenvalue of the bilaplacian
\Delta^{2}
. Furthermore, the minimiser of the associated problem is radial (provided that the conformal class of the annulus is large enough).

We believe that our ODE analysis is
universal
in the sense that it should allow us to treat a large class of elliptic operators of order
4
in all dimensions. Indeed, the algebraic systems obtained in higher dimension
d\geq 3
always reduce to one of our
2
-dimensional systems, which permits to obtain a lower estimate of the first eigenvalue of the bilaplacian in annuli in all dimension. In particular, we deduce the following weighted Poincaré estimate in all dimension.

Theorem 1.6

(Theorem C
[
44
]
)
.

Let
d\geq 3
, let
0<a<b<\infty
, and let
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.Then, for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx\geq\left(\frac{d^{2}}{4}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\left(\frac{(d-4)^{2}}{4}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx.

(1.27)

Furthermore, for all
d\geq 3
and for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)\subset W^{2,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx\geq\mu_{1}(\Omega)\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx,

(1.28)

where

\displaystyle\mu_{1}(\Omega)>\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{25+\dfrac{104\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}+\dfrac{16\pi^{4}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}{36+\dfrac{16\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}\qquad&&\text{for}\;\,d=3\\
&\frac{9+\dfrac{10\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}+\dfrac{\pi^{4}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}{3+\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}\qquad&&\text{for}\;\,d=4\\
&\frac{d^{2}(d-4)^{2}+((d-2)^{2}+4)\dfrac{8\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}+\dfrac{16\pi^{4}}{\log^{4}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}{4(d-4)^{2}+\dfrac{16\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}\qquad&&\text{for}\;\,d\geq 5.\end{aligned}\right.

Remark 1.7
.

(1)

For
d=4
, the estimate reads

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\Delta^{2},B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0))>\left(4+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}>\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)},

and the upper estimate shows that

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\Delta^{2},B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0))<\left(4+\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\underset{\frac{b}{a}\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}0,

where
\lambda_{1}>0
is the first positive eigenvalue for the weighted problem

\displaystyle\Delta^{2}u=\lambda_{1}\frac{u}{|x|^{4}}.

Notice that the power
4
is not related to the dimension but to the order of the differential operator.
This degeneracy is expected in critical dimension where concentration phenomenons occur.

(2)

Thanks to an upper bound on the first eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}(\Omega)
appearing on the right-hand side of (
1.27
), one can recover classical Rellich inequalities (in the full space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
) in dimension
d\geq 5
and
d=3
(for the subspace
W^{2,2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap\left\{u:u(0)=0\right\}
) with optimal constants. Likewise, (
1.28
) gives another proof of the Hardy-Rellich inequality with optimal constants in all dimension
d\geq 3
(see
[
62
,
9
,
10
]
).

1.3.3
Main Results

As we have explained above, due to length and technical difficulties, we restrict ourselves to the case
m=1
in the asymptotic analysis. First, let us define the notion of bubble convergence for Willmore immersions of bounded energy and possibly degenerating conformal class.

As in
[
31
]
, we can also treat the case of surfaces of degenerating conformal class, provided that a suitable bound holds on the second residue. Recall that for a Willmore immersion
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the second residue associated to the simple closed curve
\gamma
is given by (
[
42
, Equation (10)]
)

\displaystyle\vec{\gamma}_{1}(\vec{\Phi},\gamma)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\mathrm{Im}\,\int_{\gamma}\vec{\Phi}\wedge\left(\partial\vec{H}+|\vec{H}|^{2}\partial\vec{\Phi}+\,g^{-1}\otimes\langle\vec{H},\vec{h}_{0}\rangle\otimes\overline{\partial}\vec{\Phi}\right)+g^{-1}\otimes\vec{h}_{0}\wedge\overline{\partial}\vec{\Phi}.

(1.29)

Definition 1.8
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface,
\{\vec{\Phi}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of Willmore immersions from
\Sigma
into
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
g_{k}=\vec{\Phi}^{\ast}g_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
. If

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}W(\vec{\Phi}_{k})<\infty,

we say that
\{\vec{\Phi}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

bubble converges
to
(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{1},\cdots,\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{m},\vec{\Psi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\Psi}_{p},\vec{\chi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\chi}_{q})
provided that

(
1
)

The sequence
\{(\Sigma,g_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges (in the sense of Deligne-Mumford) to a nodal surface
(\widetilde{\Sigma},\widetilde{h})
of connected components
(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{\Sigma}_{m})
.

(
2
)

For all
1\leq l\leq m
, the map
\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{l}:\Sigma_{l}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a Willmore immersion such that (up to conformal transformations and diffeomorphisms in the domain)

\displaystyle\vec{\Phi}_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{1},\cdots,\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{m})\qquad\text{in}\;\,C^{r}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\Sigma\setminus\left\{p_{k}^{1},\cdots,p_{k}^{2N}\right\}\right)\;\,\text{for all}\;\,r\in\mathbb{N},

where
\left\{p_{k}^{1},\cdots,p_{k}^{2N}\right\}
converge to the
2N
(
N\in\mathbb{N}
) cusps of
\widetilde{\Sigma}
.

(
3
)

If
\Gamma_{k}=\left\{\gamma_{k}^{1},\cdots,\gamma_{k}^{N}\right\}\subset\Sigma
are the shrinking geodesics of
(\Sigma,g_{k})
, then there are no concentration points on
\Gamma_{k}
.

(
4
)

There exists a finite number of concentration points
\left\{a_{k}^{1},\cdots,a_{k}^{p}\right\}\subset\Sigma
and
\left\{b_{k}^{1},\cdots,b_{k}^{q}\right\}\subset\Sigma
such that (up to conformal maps and diffeomorphisms) for all
1\leq i\leq p
and
1\leq j\leq q

\displaystyle\vec{\Phi}_{k}(\rho_{k}^{1,i}z+a_{k}^{i})\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\vec{\Psi}_{i}\qquad\text{in}\;\,C^{r}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\widehat{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\mathscr{S}_{i}^{1}\right)\;\,\text{for all}\;\,r\in\mathbb{N},

and

\displaystyle\vec{\Phi}_{k}(\rho_{k}^{2,i}z+a_{k}^{i})\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\vec{\chi}_{j}\qquad\text{in}\;\,C^{r}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\widehat{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\mathscr{S}_{j}^{2})\;\,\text{for all}\;\,r\in\mathbb{N},

where
\{\rho_{k}^{\alpha,\beta}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,\infty)
is such that
\rho_{k}^{\alpha,\beta}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}0
and
\mathscr{S}_{\alpha}^{\beta}
are discrete subsets of
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}\simeq S^{2}
.

(
5
)

The exists integers
m_{1},\cdots,m_{q}\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}W(\vec{\Phi}_{k})=\sum_{l=1}^{m}W(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{l})+\sum_{i=1}^{p}W(\vec{\Psi}_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{q}\left(W(\vec{\chi}_{j})-4\pi m_{j}\right).

(1.30)

Remark 1.9
.

(
1
)

The difference between
\{\vec{\Psi}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq p}
and
\left\{\vec{\chi}_{j}\right\}_{1\leq j\leq q}
is that the first family comes from compact bubbles, whilst the second family is made of inverted non-compact bubbles. For example, in the case
\Sigma=S^{2}
and
W(\vec{\Phi}_{k})=16\pi
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
N. Marque (
[
32
]
) found an example where
p=0
and
q=1
, where
\vec{\vec{\Phi}}_{\infty}
is the inversion of the López surface (a minimal surface with two ends: one embedded end (
i.e.
of multiplicity
1
with zero logarithmic growth) and one end of multiplicity
3
) and
\vec{\chi}_{1}
is the inversion of the Enneper surface
\vec{\zeta}:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
, a minimal surface with a single end of multiplicity
3
. In particular, we have
W(\vec{\chi}_{1})=12\pi
,
m_{1}=3
, and

\displaystyle 16\pi=W(\vec{\Phi}_{k})\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}W(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty})+(W(\vec{\chi}_{1})-12\pi)=W(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}).

To our knowledge, no explicit example of energy quantization for higher genus surface was constructed to this day.

(
2
)

Thanks to the main theorem of
[
25
]
, the bubble convergence and the energy identity hold provided that for all
1\leq l\leq N

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\vec{\gamma}_{1}(\vec{\Phi}_{k},\gamma_{k}^{l})}{\sqrt{\mathscr{L}(\gamma_{k}^{l})}}=0,

where
\mathscr{L}(\gamma_{k}^{l})
is the length of
\gamma_{k}^{l}
with respect to the metric
g_{k}
.

Theorem A
.

Let
n\geq 3
, let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface, and assume that
\{\vec{\Phi}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
bubble converges to
(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{1},\cdots,\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{m},\vec{\Psi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\Psi}_{p},\vec{\chi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\chi}_{q})
, where all Willmore immersions are
unbranched
, and let
\left\{\gamma_{k}^{1},\cdots,\gamma_{k}^{N}\right\}\subset\Sigma
be the set of shrinking geodesics of
(\Sigma,g_{k})
. There exists a universal constant
\Lambda_{n}>0
such that the bound

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\max_{1\leq l\leq N}\frac{|\vec{\gamma}_{1}(\vec{\Phi}_{k},\gamma_{k}^{l})|}{\mathscr{L}(\gamma_{k}^{l})}\leq\Lambda_{n}

(1.31)

implies that

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left(\mathrm{Ind}_{W}(\vec{\Phi}_{k})+\mathrm{Null}_{W}(\vec{\Phi}_{k})\right)\leq\sum_{l=1}^{m}\mathrm{Ind}_{W}^{0}(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{l})+\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathrm{Ind}_{W}^{0}(\vec{\Psi}_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{q}\mathrm{Ind}_{W}^{0}(\vec{\chi}_{j})<\infty

(1.32)

where
\mathrm{Ind}^{0}_{W}=\mathrm{Ind}_{W}+\mathrm{Null}_{W}
.

Remark 1.10
.

Notice that hypothesis (
1.31
) implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\max_{1\leq l\leq N}\frac{|\vec{\gamma}_{1}(\vec{\Phi}_{k},\gamma_{k}^{l})|}{\sqrt{\mathscr{L}(\gamma_{k}^{l})}}=0.

Therefore, the analysis of
[
25
]
implies that the quantization of energy holds for our sequence of immersions. Namely,

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}W(\vec{\Phi}_{k})=\sum_{l=1}^{n}W(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{l})+\sum_{i=1}^{p}W(\vec{\Psi}_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{q}\left(W(\vec{\chi}_{j})-4\pi\hskip 1.00006ptm_{j}\right)

for some integer
m_{1},\cdots,m_{q}\geq 1
such that
4\pi\hskip 1.00006ptm_{j}\leq W(\vec{\chi}_{j})
for all
1\leq j\leq m
.

As expected, the hypotheses to get the lower semi-continuity of the Morse index (see
[
40
]
) are weaker than those of Theorem
A
, and we do not need to assume smoothness at the limit.

Theorem B
.

Let
n\geq 3
, let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface, and assume that
\{\vec{\Phi}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
bubble converges to
(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{1},\cdots,\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{m},\vec{\Psi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\Psi}_{p},\vec{\chi}_{1},\cdots,\vec{\chi}_{q})
, and let
\left\{\gamma_{k}^{1},\cdots,\gamma_{k}^{N}\right\}\subset\Sigma
be the set of shrinking geodesics of
(\Sigma,g_{k})
. Assume that

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\max_{1\leq l\leq N}\frac{|\vec{\gamma}_{1}(\vec{\Phi}_{k},\gamma_{k}^{l})|}{\sqrt{\mathscr{L}(\gamma_{k}^{l})}}=0.

(1.33)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{m}\mathrm{Ind}_{W}(\vec{\Phi}_{\infty}^{l})+\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathrm{Ind}_{W}(\vec{\Psi}_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{q}\mathrm{Ind}_{W}(\vec{\chi}_{j})\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Ind}_{W}(\vec{\Phi}_{k})

(1.34)

Remark 1.11
.

Let us point out that the definition of the Morse index is different in the case of branched immersions since normal variations are generally not integrable. Therefore, we define the Morse index in the classical sense as the number of negative vectorial variations of the second derivative.

1.3.4
Open Problems and Future Work

Besides the afore-mentioned extension of our work to branched immersions (
[
45
]
) and (extrinsic or intrinsic) biharmonic maps in dimension
4
(
[
44
]
), the most natural extension would be to the viscosity method for Willmore surfaces (
[
58
,
43
]
), namely for solutions of min-max problems related for the Willmore energy. The most famous example is the
min-max sphere eversion
(
[
24
,
14
]
), that historically consists in using the Willmore energy as a gradient flow to find the
“optimal”
sphere eversion—a sphere eversion is a continuous path of immersions linking the sphere equipped with its standard orientation to the antipodal sphere (
[
61
]
)).

Due to the
universality property
(see
[
48
]
) of the Willmore enery as the only non-trivial conformally invariant Lagrangian (since the integral of the Gauss curvature is constant by virtue of Gauss-Bonnet theorem—for the total curvature,
i.e.
the integral of the absolute value of the Gauss curvature, Tobias Ekholm (
[
12
]
) showed that the total curvature is at most
8\pi
, but it is not know whether this value is optimal or not), it seems fairly natural to use this Lagrangian as a distance function with respect to the standard embedding of the sphere. Indeed, a classical rigidity result shows that
W(\vec{\Phi})>4\pi
, unless
\vec{\Phi}:S^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
is an embedding of the round sphere (in which case
W(\vec{\Phi})=4\pi
since
H=-1
and
\mathrm{Vol}(S^{2})=4\pi
for the round metric).

Another direction is to study compactness properties provided that a bound on the Morse index is given. The results above might help us restrict the number of possible bubblings and extend the previously known compactness bounds.

1.4
Morse Index Stability in Geometric Analysis: History and Perspectives

Let us draw a short panorama of recent results related to (lower or upper) semi-continuity of the Morse index for harmonic maps and minimal surfaces.

In the Almgren-Pitts min-max theory of minimal surface, the first notable result was proven by F. C. Marques and A. Neves (
[
38
]
) who showed an upper Morse index bound of min-max minimal hypersurfaces, that can be interpreted as an lower semi-continuity result for the Morse index. One of the applications of this result is to simplify the proof of the Willmore conjecture (
[
37
]
). The proof of lower index estimates by Marques-Neves (
[
35
]
)—that corresponds to an upper-semi continuity theorem—is much more technical and uses the
Multiplicity One Conjecture
proven by X. Zhou (
[
67
]
), that asserts that for a generic set of metrics (the bumpy metrics of B. White:
[
64
,
65
]
). Marques-Montezuma-Neves (
[
34
]
) later extended this result to prove the general Morse inequalities (see
[
36
]
for more details). For the alternative construction using the Allen-Cahn functional of M. Guaraco and Gaspar-Guaraco (
[
17
,
18
]
), O. Chodosh and C. Mantoulidis showed in dimension
3
the equality between the dimension of the min-max family and the Morse index for
“bumpy metrics”
(
[
11
]
; other upper semi-continuity results include the work of F. Hiesmayr (
[
19
]
) assuming that the limiting surface is two-sided and the work P. Gaspar (
[
15
]
) in the general case).

As it appears in the afore-mentioned literature, the
Multiplicity One Conjecture
is a central property that is often crucial to obtain upper index bound. In the framework of the Allen-Cahn functional, this property was proven in the case of one-parameter families by C. Bellettini (
[
2
,
3
]
). When it comes to the
viscosity method
of T. Rivière (
[
59
]
) for the construction of (
2
-dimensional) minimal surfaces in arbitrary codimension (for which only the lower semi-continuity of the index is known
[
57
]
), the
Multiplicity One Conjecture
was proven by A. Pigati and T. Rivière (
[
51
]
; see also
[
53
]
). It is likely that the method developed in
[
31
]
will lead to a proof of the upper semi-continuity of the Morse index in the viscosity method for minimal surfaces (while the current work should apply to the viscosity method). Other natural directions would be to apply the method to the Yang-Mills-Higgs model that permits to construct codimension
2
minimal surfaces as in the work of A. Pigati and D. Stern
[
52
]
, or Morse index stability results in the viscosity method for free boundary minimal surface of A. Pigati (
[
50
]
). Finally, let us mention the work of M. Karpukhin and D. Stern (
[
22
,
23
]
; see also the earlier work of M. Karpukhin
[
21
]
) on harmonic maps that uses Morse index estimates for the Ginzburg-Landau functional and has applications to eigenvalues estimates and maximisers of the conformal volume (introduce by Li-Yau in
[
27
]
) that uses min-max constructions that enter in the framework of
min-max hierarchies

[
56
,
60
]
).

2
The Second Derivative of the Willmore Energy

Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface,
(M^{n},h)
be a complete Riemannian manifold, and let
\vec{\Phi}:\Sigma\rightarrow M^{n}
be smooth immersion. Define

\displaystyle W(\vec{\Phi})=\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{H}|^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g},

where
g=\vec{\Phi}^{\ast}h
is the induced metric, and
\vec{H}
is the mean curvature, given by

\displaystyle\vec{H}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}\vec{\mathbb{I}}_{i,j},

where
\vec{\mathbb{I}}
is the second fundamental form.

2.1
First Variation

We assume that
(M^{n},h)
is isometrically embedded into
\mathbb{R}^{m}
for
m
large enough. If
\vec{w}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R}^{m})
is any variation, let
\{\vec{\Phi}_{t}\}_{-\varepsilon<t<\varepsilon}
such that
\vec{\Phi}_{0}=\vec{\Phi}
and
\dfrac{d}{dt}\vec{\Phi}_{t}=\vec{w}
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that the following identity holds for all
-\varepsilon<t<\varepsilon

\displaystyle\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}\frac{d}{dt}\vec{\Phi}_{t}=0.

It is clear in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
since we can choose
\vec{\Phi}_{t}=\vec{\Phi}+t\,\vec{w}
.

2.1.1
Metric

In local coordinates, defining

\displaystyle g_{i,j}=\langle\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi},\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}\rangle,

we have
\det(g)=g_{1,1}\,g_{2,2}-g_{1,2}^{2}
. Therefore, if
\{\vec{\Phi}_{t}\}_{t\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)}
is a variation of
\vec{\Phi}
and
\vec{w}=\dfrac{d}{dt}\left(\vec{\Phi}_{t}\right)_{|t=0}
, by the classical symmetry lemma, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{t}=\nabla_{\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\frac{d}{dt}\vec{\Phi}_{t}.

If
\vec{w}_{t}=\dfrac{d}{dt}\vec{\Phi}_{t}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}g_{i,j}=\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\det(g_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\left(g_{1,1}g_{2,2}-g_{1,2}\right)=2\,g_{2,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+2\,g_{1,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle-2\,g_{1,2}\left(\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=2\langle d\vec{\Phi}_{t},d\vec{w}_{t}\rangle_{g_{t}}\det(g_{t}),

since

\displaystyle g^{i,j}=(-1)^{i+j}\frac{g_{i+1,j+1}}{\det(g)}

using
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
indices, which implies that for all smooth functions
\varphi,\psi:\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\langle d\varphi,d\psi\rangle_{g}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}g^{i,j}\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi\cdot\partial_{x_{j}}\psi.

We also get

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\sqrt{\det(g_{t})}=\langle d\vec{\Phi}_{t},d\vec{w}_{t}\rangle_{g_{t}}\sqrt{\det(g_{t})}.

Now, we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}g^{i,j}=-2\langle d\vec{\Phi}_{t},d\vec{w}_{t}\rangle_{g_{t}}g^{i,j}+\frac{(-1)^{i+j}}{\det(g_{t})}\left(\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i+1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{j+1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j+1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{i+1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right).

At
t=0
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}g^{1,1}=-2\,e^{-4\lambda}\left(\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\right)+2\,e^{-4\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle=-2\,e^{-4\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle,

and likewise, we find that for all
i,j=1,2

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}g^{i,j}=-e^{-4\lambda}\left(\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{j}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{i}\rangle\right).

2.1.2
Second Fundamental Form

Notice that

\displaystyle\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})=\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}=\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}-\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{\top}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}.

For all vector-fields
X,Y
, if

\displaystyle\nabla_{X}^{\top}Y=\lambda_{1}\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}+\lambda_{2}\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},

we get

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{X}Y,\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle=g_{1,1}\lambda_{1}+g_{1,2}\lambda_{2}

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{X}Y,\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle=g_{1,2}\lambda_{1}+g_{2,2}\lambda_{2}.

Therefore, we can rewrite the system as

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\langle\nabla_{X}Y,\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\\
\langle\nabla_{X}Y,\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}g_{1,1}&g_{1,2}\\
g_{1,2}&g_{2,2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}\\
\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix}

which means that

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}\\
\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}g^{1,1}&g^{1,2}\\
g^{1,2}&g^{2,2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\langle\nabla_{X}Y,\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\\
\langle\nabla_{X}Y,\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}g^{1,1}\langle\nabla_{X}Y,\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{1,2}\langle\nabla_{X}Y,\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\\
g^{1,2}\langle\nabla_{X}Y,\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{2,2}\langle\nabla_{X}Y,\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\end{pmatrix}.

In other words, we have

\displaystyle\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}-\left(g^{1,1}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{1,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}

\displaystyle-\left(g^{1,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{2,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}.

We first estimate

\displaystyle\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}=\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t}+R(\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}_{t}}\left(\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})\right)=\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\left(R(\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\right)

\displaystyle-\left(g^{1,1}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{1,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\nabla_{\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\vec{w}

\displaystyle-\left(g^{1,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{2,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\nabla_{\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\vec{w}_{t}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}w_{t}-\nabla_{\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{\top}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\vec{w}_{t}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\left(R(\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\right)

where
\pi_{\vec{n}_{t}}
is the normal projection. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{\top}\vec{w}_{t}=\left(g^{1,1}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{1,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}

\displaystyle+\left(g^{1,2}\langle\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{2,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}.

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t}=\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\vec{w}_{t}+\nabla^{N}_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\nabla^{\top}_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\vec{w}_{t}+\left(g^{1,1}\langle\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{1,2}\langle\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})

\displaystyle-\left(g^{1,2}\langle\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{2,2}\langle\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\vec{w}_{t}+\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{\top}\vec{w}_{t}).

Finally, if

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}_{X,Y}=\nabla_{X}^{N}\nabla_{Y}^{N}-\nabla_{\nabla_{X}^{\top}Y}^{N},

we get

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}_{t}}\left(\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})\right)=\nabla^{2}_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t}+\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{\top}\vec{w}_{t})+\pi_{\vec{n}_{t}}\left(R(\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\right).

(2.1)

2.1.3
Codazzi Identity

Now, using the definition of the covariant derivative, we get for all tangent vector fields

\displaystyle\nabla_{X}^{N}\vec{\mathbb{I}}(Y,Z)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{N}_{X}\left(\vec{\mathbb{I}}(Y,Z)\right)-\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\nabla^{\top}_{X}Y,Z)-\vec{\mathbb{I}}(Y,\nabla^{\top}_{X}Z)

while the Codazzi-Mainardi identity gives

\displaystyle\nabla_{X}^{N}\vec{\mathbb{I}}(Y,Z)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\perp}_{Y}\vec{\mathbb{I}}(X,Z)+\pi_{\vec{n}_{t}}\left(R(X,Y)Z\right).

2.1.4
First Derivative of the Mean Curvature

We have

\displaystyle\vec{H}_{g_{t}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}g^{i,j}\,\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}).

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}_{t}}\left(\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}\vec{H}_{g_{t}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}\left(-2\langle d\vec{\Phi}_{t},d\vec{w}_{t}\rangle_{g_{t}}g^{i,j}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{(-1)^{i+j}}{\det(g_{t})}\left(\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i+1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{j+1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j+1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{i+1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\right)\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}g^{i,j}\left(\nabla^{2}_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t}+\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{\top}\vec{w}_{t})+\pi_{\vec{n}_{t}}\left(R(\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=-\langle d\vec{\Phi}_{t},d\vec{w}_{t}\rangle_{g_{t}}\vec{H}_{g_{t}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}\frac{(-1)^{i+j}}{\det(g_{t})}\left(\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i+1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{j+1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j+1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{i+1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{g}^{N}\vec{w}_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}g^{i,j}\,\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{\top}\vec{w}_{t})+\pi_{\vec{n}_{t}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}g^{i,j}R(\vec{w},\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\right).

Notice that if
\mathscr{A}
is the Simons operator, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\vec{n}_{t}}\left(\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}\vec{H}_{g_{t}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\Delta_{g}^{N}\vec{w}+\mathscr{A}(\vec{w})+\mathscr{R}(\vec{w})\right),

(2.2)

where

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}(\vec{w})=\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}g^{i,j}R(\vec{w},\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}.

is a curvature operator.

2.2
Second Variation

2.2.1
Second Derivative of the Metric

Now, noticing that

\displaystyle\langle d\vec{\Phi}_{t},d\vec{w}_{t}\rangle_{g_{t}}d\mathrm{vol}_{g_{t}}=\frac{d}{dt}\left(d\mathrm{vol}_{g_{t}}\right),

(2.3)

we will compute the second derivative of the area form. First, since
\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}\frac{d}{dt}\vec{\Phi}_{t}=0
, and by the symmetry lemma

\displaystyle\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{t}=\nabla_{\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\frac{d}{dt}\vec{\Phi}_{t},

we get

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\left(g_{i,j}\right)_{|t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\left(\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla_{\vec{w}}\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{j}\rangle+2\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{w},\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\vec{w}}\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=2\langle\nabla_{e_{i}}\vec{w},\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{w}\rangle+\langle R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{i})\vec{w},\vec{e}_{j}\rangle+\langle R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{j})\vec{w},\vec{e}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=2\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{w},\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w}\rangle-2\langle R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{i})\vec{e}_{j},\vec{w}\rangle,

(2.4)

where we wrote
\vec{e}_{i}=\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}
, and used the symmetry of the curvature tensor. Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\left(\det(g_{t})\right)_{|t=0}=\frac{d}{dt}\left(g_{1,1}g_{2,2}-g_{1,2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=g_{2,2}\left(\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}g_{1,1}\right)+g_{1,1}\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\left(g_{2,2}\right)+2\left(\frac{d}{dt}g_{1,1}\right)\left(\frac{d}{dt}g_{2,2}\right)-2\,g_{1,2}\left(\frac{d}{dt}g_{1,2}\right)-2\left(\frac{d}{dt}g_{1,2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=2\,g_{2,2}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w}\rangle+2\,g_{1,1}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w}\rangle-4\,g_{1,2}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w}\rangle

\displaystyle-2\,g_{2,2}\langle R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1})\vec{e}_{1},\vec{w}\rangle-2\,g_{1,1}\langle R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2})\vec{e}_{2},\vec{w}\rangle-2\,g_{1,2}\langle R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1})\vec{e}_{2},\vec{w}\rangle-2\,g_{2,1}\langle R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2})\vec{e}_{1},\vec{w}\rangle

\displaystyle+8\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle-2\left(\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(2|d\vec{w}|_{g}^{2}-2\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}g^{i,j}\langle R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{i})\vec{e}_{j},\vec{w}\rangle\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{1}{\det(g)}\left(8\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle-2\left(\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\right)^{2}\right)\right)\det(g)

We see that the term

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}(\vec{w},\vec{w})=\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}g^{i,j}\langle R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{i})\vec{e}_{j},\vec{w}\rangle

is a Ricci-like curvature, where we take the Ricci curvature of
\varphi(\Sigma)\subset(M^{m},h)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, evaluated at the couple of vector-field
(\vec{w},\vec{w})
of
M^{m}
. The second component is better expressed using conformal (of complex) coordinates.
We recall that the Cauchy-Riemann operators are given by

\displaystyle\partial_{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{x_{1}}-i\,\partial_{x_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\partial_{\overline{z}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{x_{1}}+i\,\partial_{x_{2}}\right).

Therefore, we deduce that

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{1}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{z}+\partial_{\overline{z}}

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{2}}

\displaystyle=i(\partial_{z}-\partial_{\overline{z}}).

These identities imply that

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle=-\langle\partial_{z}\vec{w}+\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{w},\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}+\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{\Phi}\rangle\langle\partial_{z}\vec{w}-\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{w},\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}-\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{\Phi}\rangle

\displaystyle=-4\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi},\partial_{z}\vec{w}\rangle\right)^{2}+4\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi},\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{w}\rangle\right)^{2}

Likewise, we get

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=i\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}-\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{\Phi},\partial_{z}\vec{w}+\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{w}\rangle+i\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi}+\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{\Phi},\partial_{z}\vec{w}-\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{w}\rangle

\displaystyle=2i\left(\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi},\partial_{z}\vec{w}\rangle-\langle\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{\Phi},\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{w}\rangle\right)=-4\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi},\partial_{z}\vec{w}\rangle\right).

Therefore, we finally get

\displaystyle 4\langle{\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w}},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle-\left(\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=-16\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi},\partial_{z}\vec{w}\rangle\right)^{2}+16\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi},\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{w}\rangle\right)^{2}-16\,\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi},\partial_{z}\vec{w}\rangle\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=16\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi},\partial_{\overline{z}}\vec{w}\rangle\right)^{2}-16|\langle\partial_{z}\vec{\Phi},\partial_{z}\vec{w}\rangle|^{2}.

We finally deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\det(g_{t})=2\left(|d\vec{w}|_{g}^{2}+16|\partial\vec{\Phi}\,\dot{\otimes}\,\overline{\partial}\vec{w}|^{2}_{\mathrm{WP}}-16|\partial\vec{\Phi}\,\dot{\otimes}\,\partial\vec{w}|_{\mathrm{WP}}^{2}+\mathscr{R}(\vec{w},\vec{w})\right)\det(g),

(2.5)

where the Weil-Petersson norm of a quadratic differential
\alpha=f(z)dz^{2}
is given by

\displaystyle|\alpha|_{\mathrm{WP}}^{2}=g^{-2}\otimes\alpha\otimes\overline{\alpha}=e^{-4\lambda}|f(z)|^{2}.

Using the formula
(f(u))^{\prime\prime}=f^{\prime}(u)u^{\prime\prime}+f^{\prime\prime}(u)(u^{\prime})
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\left(\sqrt{\det(g_{t})}\right)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\det(g)}}\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\det(g_{t})-\frac{1}{4(\det(g))^{\frac{3}{2}}}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\det(g_{t})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(|d\vec{w}|_{g}^{2}+16|\partial\vec{\Phi}\,\dot{\otimes}\,\overline{\partial}\vec{w}|^{2}_{\mathrm{WP}}-16|\partial\vec{\Phi}\,\dot{\otimes}\,\partial\vec{w}|_{\mathrm{WP}}^{2}+\mathscr{R}(\vec{w},\vec{w})\right)\sqrt{\det(g)}-\frac{1}{4(\det(g))^{\frac{3}{2}}}\left(2\langle d\vec{\Phi},d\vec{w}\rangle_{g}\det(g)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(|d\vec{w}|_{g}^{2}-\langle d\vec{\Phi},d\vec{w}\rangle_{g}^{2}+16|\partial\vec{\Phi}\,\dot{\otimes}\,\overline{\partial}\vec{w}|^{2}_{\mathrm{WP}}-16|\partial\vec{\Phi}\,\dot{\otimes}\,\partial\vec{w}|_{\mathrm{WP}}^{2}+\mathscr{R}(\vec{w},\vec{w})\right)\sqrt{\det(g)}.

(2.6)

Notice that in the notations of
[
57
]
, we have

\displaystyle 16|\partial\vec{\Phi}\,\dot{\otimes}\,\overline{\partial}\vec{w}|^{2}_{\mathrm{WP}}-16|\partial\vec{\Phi}\,\dot{\otimes}\,\partial\vec{w}|_{\mathrm{WP}}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}|d\vec{\Phi}\,\dot{\otimes}\,d\vec{w}+d\vec{w}\,\dot{\otimes}\,d\vec{\Phi}|_{g}^{2}.

In fact, this expression can be simplified since in a conformal chart
\langle d\vec{\Phi},d\vec{w}\rangle_{g}^{2}=4\,\mathrm{Re}\,\left(\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{z}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{\overline{z}}\rangle\right)
, which implies that

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\left(\sqrt{\det(g_{t})}\right)_{|t=0}=\left(|d\vec{w}|_{g}^{2}-16|\partial\vec{\Phi}\,\dot{\otimes}\,\partial\vec{w}|_{\mathrm{WP}}^{2}+\mathscr{R}(\vec{w},\vec{w})\right)\sqrt{\det(g)}.

(2.7)

2.2.2
Second Derivative of the Mean Curvature

Recall that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}^{N}\vec{\mathbb{I}}(\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})=\nabla^{N}_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t}-\nabla^{N}_{\nabla^{\top}_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t}+\pi_{\vec{n}_{t}}\left(R(\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\right),

(2.8)

and
that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(g^{i,j}\right)_{|t=0}=-e^{-4\lambda}\left(\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{j}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{i}\rangle\right).

(2.9)

We have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}^{N}\nabla^{N}_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{v}_{t}=\pi_{\vec{n}_{t}}\left(\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{v}_{t}\right)-\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}^{N}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{\top}\vec{v}_{t}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{N}_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}\vec{v}_{t}+\pi_{\vec{n}_{t}}\left(R(\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})\vec{v}_{t}\right)-\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}^{N}\left(\left(g^{1,1}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{v}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{1,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{v}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\left(g^{1,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{v}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{2,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{v}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{N}_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}\vec{v}_{t}+\pi_{\vec{n}_{t}}\left(R(\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})\vec{v}_{t}\right)-\left(g^{1,1}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{v}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{1,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{v}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\nabla_{\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\vec{w}_{t}

\displaystyle-\left(g^{1,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{v}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{2,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{v}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\nabla_{\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\vec{w}_{t}.

(2.10)

Therefore, applying the identity (
2.2.2
) to
\vec{w}_{t}=\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t}
we deduce that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}^{N}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t}=\nabla^{N}_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}^{N}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\left(R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{i})\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w}\right)

\displaystyle-e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}^{N}\vec{w}-e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}^{N}\vec{w}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\left(R(\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})\vec{w}_{t}\right)+\pi_{\vec{n}}\left(R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{i})\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w}\right)-e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}^{N}\vec{w}-e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}^{N}\vec{w}.

(2.11)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\left(R(\vec{w}_{t},\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t})\vec{w}_{t}\right)=\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{j})\vec{w}+R(\nabla^{\top}_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{j})\vec{w}+R(\vec{w},\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}^{\top}\vec{e}_{j})\vec{w}+R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{j})\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}^{\top}\vec{w},

(2.12)

which shows by (
2.2.2
) and (
2.12
) that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}^{N}\left(\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t}\right)_{|t=0}=-e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}^{N}\vec{w}-e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}^{N}\vec{w}

\displaystyle+\pi_{\vec{n}}\left(\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{j})\vec{w}+R(\nabla^{\top}_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{j})\vec{w}+R(\vec{w},\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}^{\top}\vec{e}_{j})\vec{w}+R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{j})\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}^{\top}\vec{w}+R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{i})\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w}\right)

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{\nabla^{\top}\vec{e}_{i}\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w}}^{N}\vec{w}+\pi_{\vec{n}}\left(\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{j})\vec{w}+R(\nabla^{\top}_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{j})\vec{w}+R(\vec{w},\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}^{\top}\right.\vec{e}_{j})\vec{w}+R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{j})\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}^{\top}\vec{w}

\displaystyle\left.+R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{i})\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w}\right).

(2.13)

Then, we compute by (
2.2.2
) since
g^{i,j}_{|t=0}=e^{-4\lambda}\delta_{i,j}
and using (
2.9
)

\displaystyle\nabla^{N}_{\frac{d}{dt}}\nabla^{N}_{\nabla^{\top}_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\vec{w}_{t}=\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}^{N}\left(\left(g^{1,1}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{1,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\nabla_{\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\vec{w}_{t}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\left(g^{1,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+g^{2,2}\langle\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle\right)\nabla_{\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\vec{w}_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{e}_{j},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\left(\pi_{\vec{n}}\left(R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1})\vec{w}\right)-e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}^{N}\vec{w}-e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\nabla^{N}_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w}\right)

\displaystyle+e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{e}_{j},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\left(\pi_{\vec{n}}\left(R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2})\vec{w}\right)-e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}^{N}\vec{w}-e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}^{N}\vec{w}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(-2e^{-4\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{e}_{j},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle-e^{-4\lambda}\left(\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\right)\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{e}_{j},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\right.

\displaystyle\left.+e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{1}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{e}_{j},\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w}\rangle\right)\nabla^{N}_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w}

\displaystyle+\left(-2e^{-4\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{e}_{j},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle-e^{-4\lambda}\left(\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\right)\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{e}_{j},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\right.

\displaystyle\left.+e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t},\partial_{x_{2}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}\rangle+e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{e}_{j},\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w}\rangle\right)\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}^{N}\vec{w}

Since

\displaystyle\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}=\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w}+R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{i})\vec{e}_{j},

we deduce that

\displaystyle\left(\nabla_{\frac{d}{dt}}^{N}\nabla_{\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{\top}\partial_{x_{j}}\vec{\Phi}_{t}}^{N}\vec{w}_{t}\right)_{|t=0}=e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{e}_{j},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\left(\pi_{\vec{n}}\left(R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1})\vec{w}\right)-e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}^{N}\vec{w}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\nabla^{N}_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w}\right)

\displaystyle+e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{e}_{j},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\left(\pi_{\vec{n}}\left(R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2})\vec{w}\right)-e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}^{N}\vec{w}-e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}^{N}\vec{w}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(-2e^{-4\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{e}_{j},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle-e^{-4\lambda}\left(\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\right)\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{e}_{j},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\right.

\displaystyle\left.+e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\nabla_{\vec{e}_{j}}\vec{w}+R(\vec{w},\vec{e}_{i})\vec{e}_{j},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle+e^{-2\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{e}_{j},\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w}\rangle\right)\nabla^{N}_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w}

\displaystyle+\left(-2e^{-4\lambda}\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{e}_{j},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle-e^{-4\lambda}\left(\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{1}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{2}}\vec{w},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\right)\langle\nabla_{\vec{e}_{i}}\vec{e}_{j},\vec{e}_{1}\rangle\right.
